@@105240815 <p> From a parched , hot September and October -- on the brink of extreme drought -- to flooding rains in summer , the past few months have thrown up a range of weather extremes across the country . <p> Much of it comes down to the natural variability of weather in general . <p> But in the background , several climate drivers have been helping guide the weather in certain directions -- and not just El Ni ? o . <p> So how have they each been behaving recently ? <h> Indian Ocean Dipole likely helps with spring dry <p> The Indian Ocean Dipole ( IOD ) , sometimes referred to as El Ni ? o 's cousin , forms over waters to Australia 's west . <p> Exerting its influence during winter and spring , it has two phases : negative , which is the wet phase , and positive , the dry phase . <p> Loading ... <p> Since August , the IOD has been in a positive phase -- peaking at its second-highest values ever recorded by the Bureau of Meteorology , according to climatologist Catherine Ganter . <p> Based on its typical influence , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ role in the intense dry conditions experienced during winter and spring , which set up an early start to the bushfire season , and saw several regions in serious rainfall deficits . <p> This is especially true given some of the more unusual characteristics of this year 's El Ni ? o may have dampened its drying influence , according to Ms Ganter . <p> " So this positive IOD was on the strong end , and it would have likely contributed to the below-average rainfall that we saw , particularly in August to October which was that dry three month period , " she said . <p> In parts of the country , warmer-than-average temperatures have continued into summer . ( Facebook : Weather Obsessed/Helen Browne ) <p> The IOD has lingered for longer than normal this year , but is now breaking down , so is no longer having an influence on Australia 's climate , according to Ms Ganter . <h> Southern Annular Mode encourages summer rainfall <p> Though September and October were parched , rainfall kicked up a notch in November for large parts of Queensland @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Since then , the eastern states have seen repeated bouts of heavy rainfall . <p> Many parts of Victoria and South Australia have recorded an entire summer 's worth of rainfall already , and others face flooding emergencies . <p> Waterfalls are pumping in New South Wales after several heavy downpours . ( Supplied : Trudy Locke ) <p> One of the likely reasons for this enhanced summer rainfall -- particularly over the south-eastern states -- is a driver known as the Southern Annular Mode ( SAM ) , according to Monash University climate scientist Kimberley Reid . <p> The SAM refers to the north-south position of a belt of westerly winds over the southern ocean , and is linked to rainfall patterns across Australia . <p> This diagram from the Bureau of Meteorology shows the impact of the Southern Annular Mode . ( Supplied : Bureau of Meteorology ) <p> Since early December , the SAM has persistently been in a positive phase , meaning the belt of winds is closer to Antarctica , which tends to favour rainfall in summer for the eastern states . <p> " What @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it sort of draws tropical , warm , moist air further south , " Dr Reid said . <p> " It also allows for more onshore winds over the east coast , which again helps push moist air over Australia . <p> " So that 's probably why we are seeing a higher-than-average number of wet weather systems in summer this year . " <p> Something a bit unusual about the SAM 's behaviour this summer , according to Dr Reid , is that it 's happening during an El Ni ? o event , <p> Typically , a persistent positive SAM is associated with the El Ni ? o-Southern Oscillation 's opposite , phase -- La Ni ? a , which is the wet phase for Australia . <p> There have been several severe summer storms already this year . ( Facebook : Nicole Bates ) <p> Monash University climate science professor Julie Arblaster said it may have been pulled further south this year partly due to conditions in the Antarctic region , including the sea ice extent and the ozone hole . <p> " We do associate the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ think the fact that the sea ice has grown back a bit closer to normal since we 've had that positive SAM is consistent with that , " Dr Arblaster said . <p> " The other thing in the Antarctic region is that we had an ozone hole that lasted a lot longer than it usually does . <p> " That can also have an impact on a positive SAM . " <h> El Ni ? o driving warmth , but not following usual script <p> An El Ni ? o climate pattern was declared underway by the Bureau of Meteorology in September , after months of speculation . <p> It is one of the world 's most consequential climate drivers , and is linked to hotter , drier weather in Australia -- particularly during winter and spring -- with its warming influence continuing into summer . <p> Though some characteristics of the event have made it unusual , Dr Reid said the driver likely played a part in the warmer , drier conditions in spring , as well as the later onset to the monsoon in northern Australia . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ than average , according to the Bureau of Metereology . ( Facebook : Waz Powter ) <p> The event is still underway , which means it may be contributing to the warmer-than-average temperatures that have continued into summer too , along with climate change , according to Dr Reid . <p> El Ni ? o does n't have much of an influence on rainfall during summer months , which she said made the recent rainfall over eastern Australia less surprising . <p> However , there have been some signs El Ni ? o is not playing by its normal rule book this year , which Dr Reid said may have softened its overall influence . <p> " Typically during an El Ni ? o , you have cooler ocean temperatures to the east , and that 's why we often associate it with less rain , " she said . <p> " But the fact that there 's warmer ocean temperatures , and warm waters typically help promote rainfall , means that we 're likely seeing a bit more rain than we normally would if was a more traditional El @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is probably throwing climate scientists out a bit , that the patterns are n't looking like what we 're used to seeing . " <p> Cyclone Jasper was unusually early in the season for an El Ni ? o event . ( Supplied : Mark Cochrane ) <p> Dr Reid said the atmospheric component of El Ni ? o was also struggling to hold strong . <p> This was occurring at the start of the event too , which was why the Bureau was hesitant to declare the event underway in the first place . <p> " You really need both the atmosphere and ocean to be speaking the same language to get those strong impacts on Australia , " Dr Reid said . <h> Monsoon kicks off in tropics <p> Over recent weeks , heavy rainfall , gusty winds and cooler conditions have kicked in across the tropics , bringing relief to the building humidity . <p> The seasonal onset of tropical activity , known as the monsoon , was delayed this year compared to its usual late-December onset , which the Bureau said may in part be due @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 
@@105240816 <h> Sydney Festival 2024 : The five shows that stuck with us and what to see in the final week <p> From a Blak musical-cum-rock concert to cabaret with high-femme " daddy " energy , this year 's line-up does not disappoint . ( Supplied : Sydney Festival/Jacquie Manning ) <p> **45;42;TOOLONG ... Link copied <p> It 's mid-January , which means you 're back at work and whatever morsel of a break you got over summer is now but a distant memory . <p> Your overly upbeat colleague is harassing you with the latest productivity hacks and you 're one " New year , new me " quip away from going absolutely nuclear . <p> But before you do , we 'd like to gently remind those in the harbourside city* , that there is an antidote for your back-to-work doom : Sydney Festival . <p> Over the past two weeks , our team has devoured as much of the program as we can . With a week to go , here is a smattering of highlights from your faithful arts correspondents , to soothe , distract , and inspire all ye who are careening toward a new @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , we hear you . <p> *Sincere apologies to the rest of the country . <h> Big Name , No Blankets <p> " We were a band with a message and the message is for the people : Let 's all stick together , one Australia as one people , " Sammy Butcher told Nine Newspapers . ( Supplied : Sydney Festival/Brett Boardman ) <p> This show is all heart . <p> It tells the origin story of Aboriginal rock group the Warumpi Band , who became popular in the 80s and are widely known as the first rock band in Australia to sing in Aboriginal languages . <p> I did n't grow up with Warumpi Band 's music but , by the end of the show , I was converted . <p> Named after their second album , Big Name , No Blankets is a musical , but it plays like a rock concert . <p> The show was written by Andrea James with founding band member Sammy Butcher and daughter Anyupa Butcher , who have woven Luritja , Warlpiri and Gumatj/Yolngu Matha languages and culture into the story @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ years by First Nations-led ILBIJERRI Theatre Company in consultation with the Butcher family and the community of Papunya , the remote Northern Territory community where the band was formed . <p> Frontman George Burarrwanga -- played by the jaw-droppingly charismatic Googoorewon Knox -- drives much of the onstage action , while lead guitarist Sammy Butcher -- played with endearing softness by Baykali Ganambarr -- narrates the highs and lows of the band 's journey . <p> Knox sings directly to the audience as if onstage at a festival . Halfway through the show , he gets the crowd on their feet dancing . ( A rarity , if not a first , for the Roslyn Packer Theatre . ) <p> There 's an anthemic quality to many of Warumpi 's songs . ( Supplied : Sydney Festival/Brett Boardman ) <p> The show closes with a heart-rending version of My Island Home , sung by Knox in Gumatj language . The song was originally written by Warumpi guitarist Neil Murray for George Burarrwanga and was inspired by Burarrwanga 's homeland Galiwin'ku , an island off the coast of Arnhem Land . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by Christine Anu in the mid-90s , but sung in language , it hits different . It 's a moment of cultural reclamation and respect for Country . <p> The band 's enduring message of hope and resilience also shines through in iconic tracks such as Jailanguru Pakurnu and Blackfella/Whitefella . <p> " To take care of this country , we got ta be strong , " Knox sings to the crowd . It feels as relevant a message as ever . <p> As co-director Dr Rachael Maza said on opening night : " After that disastrous referendum , this is the Blak musical we all needed . " Anna Freeland <h> Anoushka Shankar <p> Anoushka Shankar brings her transcendent sitar music to the Opera House stage for one-night only . ( Supplied : Sydney Festival/Jacquie Manning ) <p> Listening to global sitar sensation Anoushka Shankar is a full-body experience ; the music seems to bypass the ears and enter straight into the body . <p> Complex , compelling and catchy , it had the chino-wearing middle-aged man to my left vigorously tapping his toes , and the 30-something woman @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ club . <p> For me , Shankar 's hypnotic blend of Indian classical sitar and contemporary genres felt like a brain massage , similar to ASMR ( that whispering internet phenomenon , known for inducing tingling pleasure sensations ) , but loud . <p> Performing in Australia for the first time since 2018 , the Grammy-nominated musician and composer sat cross-legged in billowing smoke on Sydney Opera House 's Concert Hall stage , flanked by the four other members of her London quintet . <p> Over 90 minutes , they seamlessly shifted between the syncopated rhythms of Shankar 's more energetically and politically-charged work -- such as In Her Name -- to traditional Carnatic melodies -- including a lullaby from Shankar 's maternal homeland in Southern India . <p> Watching Shankar alone had me transfixed , her left hand rapidly fingerpicking her sitar 's 20 strings with the dexterity of someone who has been playing since childhood . ( Shankar was famously taught by her father , sitar maestro Ravi Shankar . ) <p> She was visibly beaming at her onstage counterparts , who include clarinetist Arun Ghosh , bassist @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Thevararajah -- all virtuosic instrumentalists in their own right . ( Never before have I open-mouth gaped at a clarinet solo . ) <p> Afterward , I described it to a friend as : " Very , very good musicians having a very , very good time together . " <p> In a world where we 're constantly battling distraction and wandering minds , it 's rare to encounter a concert that is so compelling that you do n't want to , and simply ca n't , think of anything else . A unanimous standing ovation ended a show that I immediately wanted to watch again . Eloise Fuss <h> Smashed : The Nightcap <p> If you require delight , distraction or debauchery , look no further than Smashed : The Nightcap . <p> Co-created and MC 'd by the dangerously charming and multi-talented Adelaide cabaret artist and director Victoria Falconer , this show is a bit fun , a bit sexy and just a little bit unhinged . <p> Playing at Sydney Theatre Company 's Wharf 1 Theatre along the festival 's " Thirsty Mile " , the show @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in drag , cabaret , burlesque and circus . From Pasifika New Zealander drag star Kween Kong , of RuPaul 's Drag Race fame , to the Grammy-winning American chanteuse and Sydney Festival return act Rizo , there is no shortage of talent on the Smashed stage . <p> It 's this year 's headline cabaret-meets-circus act ; the Syd Fest line-up staple previously held by the hugely popular Briefs and typically staged in the Spiegeltent . While Smashed shares Briefs ' disarming cheek ( both varieties ) , it feels more like a cabaret show than a circus show . Expect torch songs . <p> Find out the story behind those yellow gloves at Smashed : The Nightcap . ( Supplied : Sydney Festival/Jacquie Manning ) <p> The other thing Smashed has in common with Briefs is that it 's camp -- and I mean caaaamp . <p> Falconer has taken the cabaret variety show and queered it to delicious effect . Sauntering through the crowd between songs , she dubs herself " Daddy " and demands the audience keep her martini glass full . There 's a bar @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ only too happy to oblige . <p> Amid the gaiety , there are moments of tenderness and connection , too . An honourable mention goes to Courtney Act 's confessional story about heartbreak , homophobia and one AFL player 's obsession with yellow gloves . <p> To be clear , there is also fire-breathing , hula-hooping , aerial acrobatics , a strip tease and a marshmallow stunt . ( Do n't ask . ) <p> If you get the chance , I recommend the tabled seating option -- it 's closer to the action , though I must warn you there is audience participation . Go on , try and have a bad time . Anna Freeland <h> Soliloquy <p> " It 's a deeply human pursuit , I think , trying to make a simple tune out of something that looks very humble but is capable of incredible eloquence , " Genevieve Lacey told ABC 's RN Breakfast . ( Supplied : Sydney Festival/Victor Frankowski ) <p> On the stage of City Recital Hall , a chorus of 32 untrained dancers move in unison to the honeyed tones of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ award-winning musician and recorder virtuoso Genevieve Lacey , it 's a far cry from the squeaky honks of the Australian primary school music experience -- but no less participatory . <p> The work , titled Soliloquy , is billed as a " radical re-invention of the solo recital " . In fact , there 's nothing " solo " about it . <p> The Papua New Guinea-born Australian has banded together with choreographer and director Gideon Obarzanek and dancer and choreographer Stephanie Lake to reimagine baroque composer Georg Philipp Telemann 's Twelve Fantasias for Solo Flute , which , as the name suggests , is usually played solo . <p> The result is a hybrid performance that invites " brave and curious " strangers to join Lacey on stage and experience the music through movement . <p> Speaking with ABC 's RN Breakfast , Lacey described Obarzanek 's knack for working with " untrained bodies -- normal people -- to make very simple movements that en masse look like these incredibly beautiful patterning textures " . <p> Loading ... <p> The dancers , having learned the choreography just hours before @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the recorder with flowing , gestural movements . Lacey sits with eyes closed in the centre of the group , absorbed in the Fantasias . <p> Opening up classical music to a broader audience has long been a motivation for Lacey . <p> " Often people feel like concert halls can be quite forbidding or exclusive places , " she told RN Breakfast . <p> " I hope by collaborating with dancers and filmmakers and all sorts of different people , we can welcome more and more people into this beautiful world of listening . " <p> Partway through the performance and sitting very still in my seat ( as a good classical music-goer does ) , I realised the limitations of using just my ears to experience the concert . The onstage invitation to participate felt inspiring , freeing and -- dare I say -- fun . Eloise Fuss <p> Soliloquy is set to tour Australia in 2024 , with dates and locations to be announced . <h> Gurr Era Op <p> " I thought to myself , ' What can I do ? ' and what I know @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Gela told AAP . ( Supplied : Sydney Festival/Prudence Upton ) <p> This is easily the most affecting work I 've seen this festival season . <p> The title of the work , Gurr Era Op , translates to " the face of the sea " in Meriam Mir , a language spoken in the eastern islands of the Torres Strait . <p> Blending traditional dance and storytelling with contemporary performance , it confronts the threat to Torres Strait Islander cultures posed by climate change and rising sea levels , asking the urgent question : What happens to your connection to Country if it is washed away ? <p> The work was conceived and directed by Torres Strait Islander choreographer and performer Ghenoa Gela in collaboration with Force Majeure and ILBIJERRI Theatre Company . <p> She is accompanied onstage by three other mainland-born Torres Strait Islander women -- Taryn Beatty , Aba Bero , Berthalia Selina Reuben -- who share their connections to the Torres Strait and to each other . ( They all have overlapping family ties . ) <p> " Right now I can go to that place @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ if my nephews can get to that place and I do n't know if their own kids can get there , " Gela told the National Indigenous Times . ( Supplied : Sydney Festival/Prudence Upton ) <p> Through movement , song and spoken word , they explore the compounding effects of colonialism , dispossession and climate devastation in the Torres Strait . <p> But the gravity of this very serious subject is offset by moments of levity and cultural celebration.The women playfully swap stories , recipes , songs and dances from childhood . There is a palpable sense of joy in these exchanges that makes clear the inextricable connection between Country , culture and place . <p> It 's an intimate invitation to the audience to listen deeply and consider what is at stake for Torres Strait Islander communities , and others on the frontline of the climate crisis . <p> At one point , Gela brings up the house lights and asks the audience to think about what home means to them : to conjure memories of the place ; the sounds and songs they grew up with ; @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ audience is suspended in collective imagination . <p> These moments of connection stay with you , even as they are subverted by the performers , who shift between states of contemplation , joy , grief , apprehension , anger , fear and hope . <p> At the work 's climax , the women gather up dozens of fish nets and traps set about the stage , which are made from salvaged plastic . They desperately pile them on top of each other to create a barricade , while the sound of lapping waves brings the urgency of the climate crisis crashing into the theatre . <p> It 's a call to action , and in this period of post-referendum soul-searching , it hits hard . Anna Freeland <p> Tacita Dean : One of three blockbuster shows in the Sydney International Art Series , this major survey of British artist Tacita Dean is the biggest ever held in the Southern Hemisphere . Her practice spans film , photography and printmaking and explores themes of chance , memory and time . Where : MCA . <p> Rizo : Prizmatism : If you @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ you . Returning to Syd Fest after sell-out shows in 2018 , Rizo brings her seductive pop-rock vocals , dark humour and come-hither antics to her solo cabaret show . Where : Wharf 1 Theatre , STC . <p> Overflow : From visionary trans director Dino Dimitriadis comes this re-mounted production of Travis Alabanza 's one-woman play about gender , identity and bathroom politics . Its 2022 Australian premiere made theatre history as the first Australian main stage production to have an all-trans and gender-diverse team . Where : Darlinghurst Theatre Company . <p> House of Fast Fashun : This is a DIY fashion house with runways on the hour . Facilitated by Melbourne-based art collective Fast Fashun , aspiring designers and eco-conscious consumers alike are invited to whip up an outfit from disused clothes and old textiles . Help is on hand , so grab a needle and thimble and get sewing . Where : Tumbalong Park . <p> The Chosen Haram : From London-based queer circus artist Sadiq Ali comes a unique theatrical experience , blending circus , physical theatre and traditional Chinese pole . Ali drew from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ as ex-Muslim to create the work , which explores the Islamic concept of " haram " -- things that are forbidden . Where : York Theatre , Seymour Centre . <p> Mutiara : Last , but certainly not least , is the latest work from intercultural dance theatre company Marrugeku . It centres on the little-known shared history of Malay workers and First Peoples of the Kimberley , who toiled under perilous conditions in Broome 's pearling industry , dating back to the 1800s . Where : Everest Theatre , Seymour Centre .

' <p> A successful challenge would limit the ability of government regulations ending or severely limiting America 's most protective government regulations . Instead of administrative agencies interpreting the law , this case is about whether the courts should substitute their judgement for those of experts . @@105267100 <p> Multiple people have been stung by a deadly jellyfish in the waters of a world-heritage paradise on Queensland 's coast this summer- and there are suggestions to close the area 's pristine beaches to stop more potentially life-threatening stings . <p> The dire warning from Jamie Seymour , the country 's leading authority on the deadly Irukandji jellyfish , follows six reported stingings in the waters of K'gari ( Fraser Island ) over the course of just four days this January . <p> Many have included children swimming in the pristine waters of the islands beaches or creeks . <p> The deadly Irukandji jellyfish regularly emerges over the summer season in the waters off Queensland , concentrated around the north and central coasts . Picture : NCA NewsWire <p> Professor Seymour , a professor at James Cook University 's Institute of Tropical Health and Medicine ( AITHM ) , says he has seen an increase in the length of the stinger season -- the period in which the animals emerge in Queensland waters -- over the years . <p> He first discovered the deadly jellyfish , which is only the size of a $2 coin , in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the animals ' more active season would extend from only late December to early January . <p> But in the years since , Professor Seymour says it has now extended by a few weeks to the end of January . <p> " We have seen a similar thing in Cairns where 60 years ago , the season was about one month long , " Professor Seymour said . <p> " Now , it 's close to 5-6 months long . " <p> Over January , a spate of reported Irukandji stingings resulted in emergency services racing multiple people for treatment for the suspected stings . <p> On January 13 , a child was transported to a Queensland Ambulance Service station in Happy Valley on K'gari , for a marine envenomation at 12.30am on January 13 . <p> The next day two children were flown to hospitals in Hervey Bay and Gympie for suspected marine stings near Cathedrals Beach and Woralie Creek campground . <p> Six people have been stung in the waters of K'gari ( Fraser Island ) last week alone . " <p> James Cook University 's Professor Jamie Seymour ( left , pictured on 60 Minutes ) says the length of the Irukandji 's season has increased over the years . Picture : Supplied / Facebook <p> Professor Seymour said one option was to close K'gari 's beaches to prevent more stings , so urgent research could be conducted to understand better preventive measures . <p> " The real measure we have at the moment is to close the beaches ... not a viable option , " he said . <p> " We need to ' know the enemy ' . " <p> Signs and symptoms of Irukandji stingings include severe backache or headaches , shooting pain in muscles , the chest and abdomen , nausea , vomiting , restlessness , anxiety and breathing difficulties . <p> One of 2024 's victims , an eight-year-old boy , reported losing feeling in his legs and screaming in pain while playing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , his mother Kristy Te Mana recounted the horrific moment her boy Tenzin needed to be flown to hospital for treatment . <p> " It felt like he was being stung from the inside and then within maybe five minutes he said he was complaining of chest pain and at that point , I was extremely worried , " she said . <p> " It was pretty scary , and I just tried to remain calm , " Te Mana said . <h> Our News Network <h> Our Partners <p> A NOTE ABOUT RELEVANT ADVERTISING : We collect information about the content ( including ads ) you use across this site and use it to make both advertising and content more relevant to you on our network and other sites . @@105270010 <h> Nuclear goes backwards , again , as wind and solar enjoy another year of record growth <p> The nuclear renaissance of the late-2000s was a bust due to the Fukushima disaster and catastrophic cost overruns with reactor projects . The latest renaissance is heading the same way , i.e. nowhere . Nuclear power went backwards last year . <p> Due to the ageing of the reactor fleet , the International Atomic Energy Agency ( IAEA ) anticipates the closure of 10 reactors ( 10 GW ) per year from 2018 to 2050 . <p> Thus the industry needs an annual average of 10 reactor construction starts , and 10 reactor startups ( grid connections ) , just to maintain its current output . Over the past decade ( 2014-23 ) , construction starts have averaged 6.1 and reactor startups have averaged 6.7 . <p> The number of operable power reactors is 407 to 413 depending on the definition of operability , well down from the 2002 peak of 438 . <p> Nuclear power 's share of global electricity generation has fallen to 9.2 percent , its lowest share in four decades and little more than half of its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ two decades 2004-2023 , there were 102 power reactor startups and 104 closures worldwide : 49 startups in China with no closures ; and a net decline of 51 reactors in the rest of the world . <p> In China , there were five reactor construction starts in 2023 and just one reactor startup . Put another way , there was just one reactor construction start outside China in 2023 . So much for the hype about a new nuclear renaissance . <p> Small modular reactors and ' advanced ' nuclear power <p> Small modular reactors ( SMRs ) are the subject of endless hype but there were no SMR construction starts or startups last year . The biggest SMR news in 2023 was NuScale Power 's decision to abandon its flagship project in Idaho despite securing astronomical subsidies amounting to around US$4 billion ( A$6.1 billion ) from the US government . <p> The pro-nuclear Breakthrough Institute noted in a November 2023 article that efforts to commercialise a new generation of ' advanced ' nuclear reactors " are simply not on track " and it warned nuclear advocates not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wrote : <p> " The NuScale announcement follows several other setbacks for advanced reactors . Last month , X-Energy , another promising SMR company , announced that it was canceling plans to go public . This week , it was forced to lay off about 100 staff . <p> " In early 2022 , Oklo 's first license application was summarily rejected by the Nuclear Regulatory Commission before the agency had even commenced a technical review of Oklo 's Aurora reactor . <p> " Meanwhile , forthcoming new cost estimates from TerraPower and XEnergy as part of the Department of Energy 's Advanced Reactor Deployment Program are likely to reveal substantially higher cost estimates for the deployment of those new reactor technologies as well . " <p> Nuclear decline vs. record renewables growth <p> The International Energy Agency ( IEA ) has just released its ' Renewables 2023 ' report and it makes for a striking contrast with the nuclear industry 's malaise . <p> Nuclear power suffered a net loss of 1.7 GW capacity in 2023 , whereas renewable capacity additions amounted to a record 507 GW , almost @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ year in a row that renewable capacity additions set a new record , the IEA states . Solar PV alone accounted for three-quarters of renewable capacity additions worldwide in 2023 . <p> Nuclear power accounts for a declining share of share of global electricity generation ( currently 9.2 percent ) whereas renewables have grown to 30.2 percent . The IEA expects renewables to reach 42 percent by 2028 thanks to a projected 3,700 GW of new capacity over the next five years in the IEA 's ' main case ' . <p> The IEA states that the world is on course to add more renewable capacity in the next five years than has been installed since the first commercial renewable energy power plant was built more than 100 years ago . <p> Solar and wind combined have already surpassed nuclear power generation and the IEA notes that over the next five years , several other milestones will likely be achieved : <p> -- In 2025 , renewables surpass coal-fired electricity generation to become the largest source of electricity generation <p> -- In 2025 , wind surpasses nuclear electricity generation <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> -- In 2028 , renewable energy sources account for over 42 percent of global electricity generation , with the share of wind and solar PV doubling to 25 percent . <p> An estimated 96 percent of newly installed , utility-scale solar PV and onshore wind capacity had lower generation costs than new coal and natural gas plants in 2023 , the IEA states . <p> " Prior to the COP28 climate change conference in Dubai , the International Energy Agency ( IEA ) urged governments to support five pillars for action by 2030 , among them the goal of tripling global renewable power capacity . Several of the IEA priorities were reflected in the Global Stocktake text agreed by the 198 governments at COP28 , including the goals of tripling renewables and doubling the annual rate of energy efficiency improvements every year to 2030 . Tripling global renewable capacity in the power sector from 2022 levels by 2030 would take it above 11 000 GW , in line with IEA 's Net Zero Emissions by 2050 ( NZE ) Scenario . <p> " Under existing policies and market conditions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by 2028 . This growth trajectory would see global capacity increase to 2.5 times its current level by 2030 , falling short of the tripling goal . " <p> In the IEA 's ' accelerated case ' , 4,500 GW of new renewable capacity will be added over the next five years ( compared to 3,700 GW in the ' main case ' ) , nearing the tripling goal . <p> Tripling nuclear ? <p> The goal of tripling renewables by 2030 is a stretch but it is not impossible . Conversely , the ' pledge ' signed by just 22 nations at COP28 to triple nuclear power by 2050 appears absurd . <p> The Labor federal government signed Australia up to the renewables pledge but not the nuclear pledge . The Coalition wants to do the opposite , and also opposes the Labor government 's target of 82 per cent renewable power supply by 2030 . <p> One of the lies being peddled by the Coalition is that nuclear power capacity could increase by 80 percent over the next 30 years . That is based on a ' high @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's ' low case ' scenario -- ignored by the Coalition -- is for another 30 years of stagnation . <p> So should we go with the IAEA 's high or low scenarios , or split the difference perhaps ? <p> According to a report by the IAEA itself , the Agency 's ' high ' forecasts have consistently proven to be ridiculous and even its ' low ' forecasts are too high -- by 13 percent on average . <p> Nuclear power wo n't increase by 80 percent by 2050 and it certainly wo n't triple ; indeed it will struggle to maintain current output given the ageing of the reactor fleet and recent experience with construction projects . <p> Comparing nuclear and renewables in China <p> China 's nuclear program added only 1.2 GW capacity in 2023 while wind and solar combined added 278 GW . Michael Barnard noted in CleanTechnica that allowing for capacity factors , the nuclear additions amount to about 7 terrawatt-hours ( TWh ) of new low carbon generation per year , while wind and solar between them will contribute about 427 TWh annually @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ One of the things that western nuclear proponents claim is that governments have over-regulated nuclear compared to wind and solar , and China 's regulatory regime for nuclear is clearly not the USA 's or the UK 's . They claim that fears of radiation have created massive and unfair headwinds , and China has a very different balancing act on public health and public health perceptions than the west . They claim that environmentalists have stopped nuclear development in the west , and while there are vastly more protests in China than most westerners realize , governmental strategic programs are much less susceptible to public hostility . <p> " And finally , western nuclear proponents complain that NIMBYs block nuclear expansion , and public sentiment and NIMBYism is much less powerful in China with its Confucian , much more top down governance system . <p> " China 's central government has a 30 year track record of building massive infrastructure programs , so it 's not like it is missing any skills there . China has a nuclear weapons program , so the alignment of commercial nuclear generation with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a strong willingness to finance strategic infrastructure with long-running state debt , so there are no headwinds there either . <p> " Yet China ca n't scale its nuclear program at all . It peaked in 2018 with 7 reactors with a capacity of 8.2 GW . For the five years since then then it 's been averaging 2.3 GW of new nuclear capacity , and last year only added 1.2 GW ... "

@@105291403 <h> Three reasons tropical Aussie forests are becoming unrecognisable <h> Trees in Queensland 's beautiful Wet Tropics can disappear under a weighty vine in just one year . <p> Vines are swamping forests around Australia 's Wet Tropics , stunting the growth of trees before they can reach their full height . The rapidly changing climate has tipped conditions in favour of woody liana that chokes low-elevation foliage below . <p> It means that in just a year , fast-growing vines can grow over a tree . Their weight is often so great that trunks beneath will bend and break . Professor Andy Marshall from the University of Sunshine Coast 's Forest Researcher Institute explained the vines transform the regions they take over into something " very different " to what most people think of as forests . <p> " If you look at them , you can see isolated trees , or the crowns of some trees popping out for the top of the vines , but essentially it 's a big mass of vines and thorny vegetation that 's about the tenth the height of a regular forest , " he told Yahoo . " For all @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , it looks like a thicket . " <p> Other hotspots for vines were low-elevation forests in East Africa , Vietnam , Colombia . Under the dense growth they are humid and filled with bugs . <p> " We have to get on our hands and knees and get underneath the vegetation to mark and measure the vines . There 's a lot of marsh flies , a lot of mosquitoes , even birds . It 's quite lively , " Marshall said . " Although these places look very unhealthy , the vines do actually help maintain biodiversity that can help the forest in the future . " <h> Three conditions causing low-lying forests to be consumed by vines <p> Forests in Queensland 's Wet Tropics region were among 556 locations studied across 44 countries over 20 years by researchers . They identified three key factors that are helping the vines outcompete other plants : <p> A mean annual temperature above 27.8 degrees . <p> Rainfall is less than 1614 mm . <p> Forest already disturbed by logging . <p> Researchers found the vine is better able to deal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ invasive plant swamps trees below , they are unable to soak up and store carbon . While the vines can be enormous , they do n't have the same " massive " trunks as the trees they outcompete and therefore ca n't store as much carbon . <p> The question scientists must now consider is what to do with the vines . Cutting them could help restore biodiversity of foliage , but rapidly removing them could hard the creatures that have evolved to live beneath them . <p> More disturbance of forests is expected to continue , particularly around Queensland . The state is home to some of the most extensive land clearing in the developed world , and it is increasingly being impacted by severe cyclones that can impact forests because of climate change . <p> The Indian prime minister hailed today 's opening of a new Hindu temple -- but it 's been fraught . Ravi Agrawal , editor in chief of Foreign Policy magazine , and a former CNN New Delhi Bureau chief , explains why . 
@@105291404 <h> In 2019 and 2020 extreme bushfires destroyed thousands of hectares of bushland . The nation 's wildlife is still recovering . <p> More of Australia 's wild places , creatures and plants were added to the nation 's threatened species list than ever before in 2023 . The total of 144 was five times the yearly average , and twice as high as 2019 , the previous record year , analysis from the Australian Conservation Foundation ( ACF ) has revealed . <p> Although listing species on the Commonwealth 's Environment Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act is designed to boost protections , some animals have found themselves slipping closer towards extinction . <p> One of them was the southern emu-wren , which was up-listed from vulnerable to endangered in July , with habitat destruction and fragmentation , bushfires and climate change all listed as threats . The Department of Environment is currently considering an application to build a rocket launching site on one of its last remaining strongholds near Port Lincoln in South Australia . <p> In 2022 , high profile species including the greater glider and koala were listed as endangered , but those protected in 2023 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in November , the Pedra Branca skink lives at a tiny island that separated from Tasmania 15,000 years ago . The island itself is 2.5 hectares , but only 0.14 hectares of it are suitable habitat for the lizard . No more than 600 remain and climate change , and increased predation by silver gulls are both threats to their survival . <p> Also included among the 144 are 17 species that were added in December , including the Mertens ' water monitor and Mitchell 's water monitor which have been impacted by the spread of cane toads as well as habitat destruction from agriculture , mining and housing developments . <h> Urgent call for Australia 's nature laws to be overhauled <p> Australia has the worst mammalian extinction record in the world , ACF nature campaigner Peta Bulling said the high number of listings shows Australia 's nature protection laws are failing . <p> ACF 's Peta Bulling noted 10,426 hectares of habitat destruction was approved under Australia 's nature protection laws , but much more was likely cleared without approval . <p> " We urge Environment Minister Tanya @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ law deals with the problem of habitat destruction , which keeps pushing unique and much-loved Australian species towards extinction , " she said . <p> Two brothers who filmed themselves torturing animals and " terrorised " residents in the Hampshire area have been jailed for a total of eight years and eight months . @@105294600 <p> The recent disastrous storms in Australia ( in Queensland , NSW and Wester Australia ) were all major threats to economic life , such was their impact . Such storms are largely unpredictable as they are caused by climate change and meteorologists do n't have their models calibrated to deal with this new data . <p> We can expect many more such events as runaway excessive heat in our atmosphere continues . So what can we do to prepare whilst going net zero ? <p> The biggest impacts of the recent storms were on transmission lines , which are not able to cope with these new high intensity storms and which can take weeks to fix when they come down . <p> Source : Western Power . <p> Transmission lines bring power from power plants in regional areas to centres of economic life . They are critical to the old fossil fuel grids and are seen by many as critical for bringing solar and wind farm power to towns , cities and especially heavy industries . But we need a more resilient system . <p> Two new studies from Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory in California have shown there @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ research teams examined the economic feasibility of this new technology and then how they could be used to improve resilience in emergencies and as a means of replacing transmission lines as well as . <p> The studies by teams led by Popovich in 2021and Moraski in 2023 were published in Nature Energy and found that battery freight trains can tap into the new highly cost effective solar and wind power technologies ( around half the price of diesel ) and replace diesel locomotives with battery powered locomotives that enable regenerative braking . <p> The second paper looked at how the battery freight trains could also be used to bring extra batteries in containers into emergency situations so that the train system played a vital role in resilience planning and in the grid integration to replace back up power and firming that is the basis for continued use of gas turbines . <p> That paper concluded : <p> ' The potential to use the US rail system as a nationwide backup transmission grid over which containerized batteries , or rail-based mobile energy storage ( RMES ) , are shared among regions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ resilience . <p> We find that RMES is a feasible reliability solution for low-frequency , high-impact events and quantify its cost effectiveness relative to reliability-driven investments in transmission infrastructure and stationary capacity . <p> Compared to new transmission lines and stationary battery capacity , deploying RMES for such events could save the power sector upwards of US$300 per kW-year and US$85 per kW-year , respectively . ' <p> Thus , these RMES systems applied along a transmission corridor may replace the need for transmission lines altogether . <p> The concept is that trains can not only quickly provide large extra stored power in large quantities to any site on the rail network providing the resilience needed but they can also be the missing link in the transition period to a net zero economy . <p> If battery freight trains were fitted with a whole-train set of battery bogies that are filled with solar or wind power from DER or large solar/wind farms , they could be distributed along the line to all the necessary industries , towns , cities and ports that are necessarily requiring net zero power to make @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ eventually remove the need for transmission lines in the long term . <p> To best understand how this could work I believe we would need to assume a full distributed energy package is still used as much as possible in all local areas and local industries , but rather than rely on gas turbines as back-up the idea is that mobile renewable power , could be stored in batteries and then delivered wherever it was needed by trains as a utility service . They can become the missing link in our net zero economies . <p> How can this happen ? Its best understood by applying it to a local situation . <p> Application to Australia <p> The freight train network in Australia carries minerals , agricultural products and increasingly processed materials such as the new critical minerals so necessary for the decarbonization of the world . <p> The addition of a mobile battery storage system that can become rapidly applied to disaster relief as well as being part of the net zero production system , can be demonstrated in Western Australia where battery freight trains are already being purchased @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ zero mining industry . <p> Image : PilbaraRailways.com.au <p> There are two parts of WA that would immediately benefit by this new model : the South West Integrated Sytem ( SWIS ) where a recent massive , unpredicted storm destroyed the transmission system going out to Kalgoorlie , and the North West Integrated System ( NWIS ) being built to join up multiple mining systems in the cyclone-sensitive mining area of the Pilbara . <p> South West Integrated System <p> The SWIS is a highly spread-out grid and two processes are causing a rapid decarbonisation of the grid : Stand-alone Power Systems in rural weak parts of the grid ; and a huge growth in rooftop solar in Perth and regional cities . <p> These provide the baseline power during the day with increasing use of wind in some areas , and increasing battery storage options in community scale and industrial scale . There is an increasing need for batteries to provide daily firming that replace gas turbines and these batteries will be needed for industries as well as towns across the whole region . <p> Battery freight trains can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in providing extra batteries into the system wherever needed . <p> They will need recharging and swapping of batteries at major sites in the system and thus a large number of batteries can be provided as back up in any regional and urban locations . Many country town stations have space for these mobile storage systems . <p> These stores of batteries can then be available for immediate relief in areas where power is down . Back-up power is already part of the SWIS grid system but the gas turbines in Kalgoorlie , which are not used for years as they are paid to come on only in emergencies , could not be used as the transmission lines were down . <p> A battery-freight train storage system can be developed along each transmission line in the SWIS working with the needs of local industries and towns , and helping each place and processing centre to create a net zero economy . <p> The regional ports of Geraldton , Bunbury , Albany and Esperance are all moving towards net zero and are obvious places for greater processing of mineral and agricultural @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The inland port of Kalgoorlie has a large rail network linking it to mineral and agricultural areas with many nearby solar and wind farms . This place could be a major centre for battery freight train systems to be focussed and would have prevented the disastrous impact from the recent storm . <p> The new port of Fremantle ( Westport in Kwinana ) is being created as a net zero port and has close links to the Kwinana Industry Complex which is also going net zero . The large rail network feeding into this could be the first place to be a service centre battery-freight train storage , recharge and maintenance area . <p> The whole system could then show how DER works . The local distribution system with its local solar would have lots of battery back-up so it 's the baseload service , along with smart firming of the system from batteries . <p> Solar farms and wind farms in the regions can then be stored in batteries and then be brought into cities and towns by train across the grid when needed . <p> The idea @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ water system where wind power-based desalination has replaced rain-fed dams and groundwater for much of the region . <p> But the original transmission pipelines to Kalgoorlie and the South West are still used after pumping the coastal desal water up the scarp to the original transmission dam built over 120 years ago . <p> The train lines followed over the next hundred years and opened up the regions for agriculture and rural communities . Now the train lines could begin to service the net zero economy . <p> North West Integrated System <p> The NWIS has been planned for many years but mining companies have only recently taken steps to be joined into a regional power service . This will enable the whole system to rapidly become renewable as the Pilbara has the highest solar energy levels in the world . <p> The rail systems are highly developed for the mining industry and could easily carry more battery storage to enable each place to work without large transmission systems that are no longer needed when the mine closes . The rail system can carry extra batteries to areas damaged by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are likely to be developed for battery electric trains in storage , recharge and maintenance . These are also likely to become places for processing net zero products . <p> Already there is a project to underground local distribution power lines and if transmission lines can be replaced by battery electric trains then the whole area becomes far more resilient as well as net zero . <p> The Pilbara can be a model for many parts of the Middle East and Africa where solar and wind are being developed for their future but expensive and inefficient transmission lines are a big challenge to going net zero . <p> Conclusion <p> There is much happening with solar and batteries in homes and stand-alone power systems and now starting in urban precincts as part of net zero strategies . <p> But the issue remains about grid integration which can help not just in enabling the removal of the last gas turbines , but also how to respond to the growing need for emergency provision of power to areas that need immediate power assistance . <p> Battery-electric freight trains clearly have a role @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rail transport systems that already service industries in regions , could be used in emergencies to bring emergency batteries into areas that need them due to climate change-induced destruction of transmission lines . <p> These growing supplies of EV trains and batteries could then be stored along the rail lines to create a business model that replaces the need for transmission lines altogether over a longer term .

@@105348969 <h> World increasingly underprepared for ' overlooked ' weather problem <h> ' Excess humidity is really preventing the body from cooling down by evaporating our sweat . ' <p> Australians are among the one billion people whose homes will become unliveable because of humidity . But researchers warn authorities are " underprepared " when it comes to tackling the threat . <p> There has been very little climate monitoring in tropical cities , and most of the weather stations have been set up in North America and Europe which are n't as impacted by humidity . This has led to a bias that focuses on heat rather than humidity , Monash University 's Dr Emma Ramsay has warned . <p> Measuring heat alone does not give a true picture of how stifling conditions are , because when humidity is also high it makes it harder still to stay comfortable . " Excess humidity is really preventing the body from cooling down by evaporating our sweat . If it 's so hot that your body temperature starts to rise , and you ca n't cool down because it 's too humid , then you 're pushed into heatstroke , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by climate researchers . Source : Getty <p> Earlier this month Sydney struggled through its most humid day on record . On January 11 the dew point , a measure of humidity , hit 26.7 degrees the highest ever recorded at the Sydney 's Observatory Hill . <p> 2023 was the world 's hottest year on record because of climate change , and global average temperatures pushed beyond 1.5 degrees above pre-industrial levels for the first time . That 's particularly a problem for parts of sub-Saharan Africa and South Asia that are already hot , which are expected to be the first parts of the world to face extended periods of heat beyond human tolerance . <p> At 2 degrees of warming , parts of northern Australia will face the same problem . " Not all the time , but for extended periods of more than a few hours . Places could experience this multiple times a year in a way that 's really going to impact how we live , and if we can live there , " Ramsay said . <h> Why air conditioning is n't the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the poor and those who work outside . " Some of us might say : Oh well , we 'll stay inside , we have air conditioning . But this option is n't available to the vast majority of the world who will have the highest exposure to these kinds of conditions , " Ramsay added . <p> " Large scale uptake of air conditioning , which we 're certainly already seeing , is not going to be a sustainable or an equitable outcome for anybody , " Ramsay said . <p> " We need to be also considering if our power grids will be able to handle this . When there are blackouts during heatwaves this causes huge amounts of mortality . So air conditioning ca n't be seen as a catch-all fix . " <p> A man has been arrested after confessing that he started a fire in South Africa that killed 76 people - while trying to get rid of a body . The 29-year-old made the shock confession during a public inquiry into the cause of the fire at a five-storey building in Johannesburg in August @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ he had strangled someone to death in the basement of the block of flats , before setting fire to his body , according to South African media reports of the testimony .

@@105350062 <h> Subscribe now for unlimited access . <p> Tick numbers are high and likely to increase , as climate change is continuing to deliver us with hotter , more humid weather than happens to favour ticks . <p> There are also social factors at play . <p> After years of pandemic associated movement restrictions , many Australians are going to the bush and coast over summer , and bringing their animals along . <p> During the pandemic , we saw a huge increase in the number of people adopting pets , and - in particular , a big increase in the number of first-time dog owners . These owners may be less familiar with the risks posed by paralysis ticks . <p> It can be tricky to choose the right tick preventative , so it is important to seek veterinary advice . <h> Here are some key questions <p> Is the preventative that I am using appropriate for my pet 's age , health and body weight ? <p> Changes in weight - especially in growing animals - can mean that a tick preventative dose that was once effective is no longer enough to protect an animal . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cat ? <p> For example , if you have a pet that spits out tablets or wo n't touch chewable preventatives , a spot-on or collar may be more suitable . <p> How long is the product I am using active against the paralysis tick ? <p> There is a large variation in the duration of tick prevention offered by different products . <p> A common mistake I see is when owners who buy a three-pack of monthly preventatives - i.e. one tablet to be given each month - assume each single tablet in the packet lasts for three months . <p> How does the preventative I use fit with other preventatives I use ? <p> If you 've been using a tick product that incorporates a heartworm preventative , but switch the tick product , you may find your pet is no longer covered for heartworm . <p> Heartworm is spread by mosquitoes and our weather conditions mean their activity and range has also expanded . <p> It is critical to understand the limitations of products . While these products are highly effective , a small number of animals @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It is important , when you are living in or visiting an area where ticks are found , to check your pet for ticks each day . <p> Look in the areas where it is hard for animals to remove them - inside the ears , the nose , in the lip folds and mouth , around the neck and shoulders . <p> If you find a tick , remove it immediately using a tick twister or , if not sure , go to a vet immediately . <p> The toxin is quite potent and will cause progressive signs , so you should not wait . <p> Withhold food and water until you talk to a vet , as there is a risk of aspiration pneumonia ( paralysis of the swallowing apparatus such that saliva , water and food might be inhaled into the lungs instead of swallowed ) . <p> Remember animals can develop symptoms even 24-48 hours after a tick has been removed . <p> Dr Anne Quain is a lecturer at the Sydney School of Veterinary Science and a practising veterinarian.

@@105351562 <p> Floating solar PV capacity is expected to play a " critical role " in fulfilling Southeast Asia 's overall solar expansion , with countries such as the Philippines , Indonesia , and Thailand well positioned to use their unique geographies to support the technology . <p> This is the key finding from a new analysis from independent energy research firm , Rystad Energy , which predicts that Southeast Asia will account for 10% of the broader Asia region 's total solar capacity by 2030 -- including ground-mounted , rooftop , and floating . <p> But unlike the rest of Asia , where land for ground-mounted solar projects is more frequently available , available land in Southeast Asia is generally set aside for agricultural purposes , leading solar developers to look offshore for potential solar locations . <p> Nine of the world 's top ten largest floating solar projects are currently located in either China or Taiwan . <p> Many more projects , however , are being proposed for the south-east Asia region , building on the example of the 145MW Cirata floating solar PV project in West Java , Indonesia which was commissioned in November 2023 and is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a number of smaller floating solar PV projects across Southeast Asia , making up the 500MW worth of operational floating solar PV projects already in operation across Southeast Asia . <p> " FPVs have emerged as a game-changer for Southeast Asia , catalysing the region 's push towards clean energy by maximizing its abundant solar resources and overcoming limited land availability , " said Jun Yee Chew , Head of Asia renewables and power research at Rystad Energy . <p> " Additionally , with land rights a major deterrent facing solar developers in Southeast Asia , as much of the land is used for agriculture , FPVs provide a solution for the coexistence of solar farms and agriculture . " <p> The pipeline of solar projects proposed across Southeast Asia out to 2030 is dominated by ground-mounted and rooftop solar , but more and more countries are looking to add floating solar PV by the end of the decade . <p> Thailand is currently serving as a potential example for other countries in the region , relying primarily on private power purchase agreements ( PPAs ) to contract floating solar @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ solar leasing model . <p> This model serves as an example for other developers and utilities looking to navigate the balance between agricultural needs and expanding renewable energy generation . <p> Moreover , relying on private leasing and PPAs helps to ensure developers are not scrambling to create more land for use which , in a region densely populated by rainforests , helps to avoid further deforestation . <p> Rystad Energy expects to see Indonesia , the Philippines , and Thailand lead Southeast Asia 's push into floating solar . <p> The Philippines is particularly well suited to develop floating solar PV , able to make use of the many large inland lakes which predominant across their archipelago , such as Laguna Lake , which Rystad Energy claims boasts nearly 3GW of capacity . <p> The Philippines-based Acen is on track to become the leading floating solar PV developer in the region by the end of the decade . Already the company is working to commission a 1GW project on Laguna Lake , along with a 200MW project in the Filipino province of Rizal . <p> When these two projects @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ floating solar PV portfolio in Southeast Asia in excess of 3GW . <p> Similarly , Indonesia 's high use of hydropower could be complemented with floating solar PV , matching the company 's solar ambitions . The country already has a 1.8GW floating solar PV project set to be built at the Duriangkang reservoir in Batam , which is being led by Spanish-headquartered EDP Renewables . <p> Joshua S. @@105351564 <p> Building on recent momentum to kick start a lagging utility-scale solar industry , a new solar farm near Te Aroha on the North Island has been announced which , upon completion in 2026 , will be New Zealand 's largest . <p> Global renewable energy infrastructure developer Harmony Energy Limited has entered into a joint venture with First Renewables , part of New Zealand energy group Clarus , to develop and own what will be -- at least for a time -- the biggest solar farm in the country . <p> The 150MW Tauhei solar project will be built on 182 hectares near Te Aroha in the Waikato region of New Zealand , and will consist of approximately 330,000 solar panels and is expected to generate over 270GWh each year . <p> The project is still subject to obtaining necessary consents and approvals , but if it goes ahead , Harmony Energy expects the complete construction and commissioning in 2026 , at which point it will supply power for the equivalent needs of around 35,000 New Zealand homes . <p> A final investment decision is expected to be taken by the two companies later this year , with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Harmony Energy director Peter Grogan said the project will create opportunities for local businesses , employment and deliver significant biodiversity gains , and will allow for continued farming production . <p> The buildout of utility-scale solar has been slow in New Zealand , with the country 's first utility scale solar farm only completed in November of 2023 . <p> This latest announcement comes only days after New Zealand power company Genesis Energy and joint venture partner FRV Australia announced that they have reached financial close on a 63MW solar farm to be built at Lauriston on the Canterbury Plains . <p> Genesis and FRV Australia are also in the process of assessing three sites on New Zealand 's North Island which would add up to a combined capacity of up to 400MW . <p> Joshua S. @@105351625 <h> Regulators urged to tackle Toyota over use of " electric " in hybrid car ads <p> Regulators in Australia and around the world are being urged to take Toyota to task over the way it advertises hybrid vehicles . <p> The US-based group , Eko , issued the call on Tuesday , claiming an analysis of Toyota websites in 23 countries showed the company used terms such as " electrified " and " electric " in its advertisements in ways that could mislead consumers . <p> The group 's report found a website dedicated to Toyota " electrified " vehicles had yet to list any battery electric cars , including its first that is due for release in Australia in February . <p> But a Toyota Australia spokesman said consumers understood its advertisements for hybrid vehicles and argued electric vehicles could not " be the automotive industry 's only answer to climate change " . <p> The report from Eko , titled Electrifraud , analysed 25 of Toyota 's websites across 23 countries in January , including Australia . <p> It found Toyota 's websites repeatedly used words including " electrification " and " electrified " on pages @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ vehicles , which the group claimed was an attempt to " capitalise on growing electric vehicle demand to sell more polluting vehicles " . <p> The report alleged the metadata on some Toyota websites had " misled Google 's AI into conflating hybrids with electric vehicles " and resulted in incorrect search results . <p> Its authors noted Toyota 's " electrified " website had yet to list the company 's first battery electric vehicle , the bZ4X , and instead featured only hybrid and hydrogen cars . <p> Eko campaigner Eoin Dubsky said the analysis proved the company was " greenwashing " advertising campaigns and called on Toyota to end " bait-and-switch tactics " to sell hybrid vehicles . <p> " Toyota simply has to change , " he said . <p> " Transport accounts for around 20 per cent of global carbon dioxide emissions ... ( and ) about 75 per cent of this is from road transport . " <p> The report called on regulators including the including the Australian Competition and Consumer Commission , European Commission , UK Advertising Standards Authority and US Federal Trade Commission @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ACCC to " urgently investigate the complaint about misleading advertising " filed by Greenpeace in March 2023 . <p> The complaint made seven claims about Toyota 's advertising , alleging its plans to reach net-zero by 2050 were misleading and that its hydrogen-powered cars should not be classified as " low-emission vehicles " . <p> A Toyota Australia spokesman told AAP the brand had chosen to back hybrid vehicles as cars " for the masses " because they were cheaper to produce than electric cars , used less petrol than traditional vehicles and would not put significant strain on mineral resources . <p> " Battery electric vehicles can not be the automotive industry 's only answer to climate change now , " he said . <p> Toyota remained the top-selling vehicle brand in Australia in 2023 , selling more than 215,000 new vehicles , according to the Federal Chamber of Automotive Industries . <p> Note : The Eko report notes that the lifecycle emissions of hybrids and plug-in hybrid vehicles are much closer to polluting conventional petrol cars than to battery electric vehicles . , however a researcher from Murdoch University believes investors should take caution before throwing their financial support behind the supplement . <p> While only a relatively recent discovery , Australian research into the seaweed has already attracted tens of millions of dollars from investors , with Tasmanian-based company , Sea Forest supported by $34m . <p> But is it too good to be true ? <p> Marine scientist and PhD candidate Ben Camer-Pesci was drawn to the stunning benefits shown from research around asparagopsis , however during a literature review , he quickly discovered some major flaws , and said the technology was " nowhere near " a point of being safely implemented . <p> " I 'm very worried about it , because a lot of people are talking about it and companies are already advertising it as a solution @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Mr Camer-Pesci said . <p> He looked at more than 90 journal articles from across the globe in order to form his review article , which distinguishes between fact and sensationalism . <p> Trials showed evidence of digestive problems , including ulcerations , haemorrhaging , and inflammation throughout the walls of the cow 's rumen . <p> Mr Camer-Pesci also noted reports of cows refusing feed with asparagopsis supplements , especially in high concentrations . <p> It 's believed that the poor taste of asparagopsis comes from the chemical , bromoform , which is naturally occurring in seaweed . <p> Research also found that milk production not only decreased , but also contained higher levels of bromoform . <p> Bromoform is categorised as a ' probable ' human carcinogen , which means there is n't enough evidence to give it a higher category in humans , however there had been reports of how it affected animals . <p> According to some research , the levels of bromoform reported in the milk was below the maximum human intake . <p> Mr Camer-Pesci said in his review the potential effects of asparagopsis @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ when moving forward with the technology . <p> He said the amount of " bandwagoning " behind the seaweed was concerning , particularly as many were unlikely to look deeper into the research available . <p> While his reading covered a range of topics , Mr Camer-Pesci noticed there were many more facets to the technology which lack research , or are missing entirely . <p> Mr Camer-Pesci said hydrogen was created by microbes in the rumen , which is converted to methane gas in a process called methanogenesis . <p> When methane is reduced , hydrogen builds up in the rumen , which can cause inflammation , weight loss and a reduced appetite . <p> These symptoms were also observed across the literature . <p> While weight gain was affected , there was n't any evidence within the literature to suggest differences in meat quality or taste . <p> Another major gap Mr Camer-Pesci noticed across the available research was that animal husbandry practices used in the trials were considerably different to what would be used onfarm . <p> " A lot of the time these trials are carried @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ chamber that measures the gas output , and this can be a very stressful situation for an animal that is used to living in a herd and roaming freely , so this can impact digestive function , " Mr Camer-Pesci said . <p> " That 's something that I feel needs to have more research put into it . " <p> He also found little conversation around producing the seaweed . <p> " Growing asparagopsis is extremely difficult , and to grow it in the volumes required to make a notable impact ... the volume of biomass required is astronomical , " Mr Camer-Pesci said . <p> While asparagopsis still shows promise in reducing methane emissions , Mr Camer-Pesci said there still had to be some " serious research and consideration " before taking the technology further . <p> He likened the buzz around asparagopsis to that of biodiesel in the mid 2010s . <p> " That ( biodiesel ) was an amazing thing , and a few years into it , they hit a wall and it could n't go any further - I think it 's very much @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " It 's been an incredible discovery at a time when we really need an answer to a climate change problem , and everyone jumped on it . <p> " Unfortunately , after conducting the literature review , I did n't want to work with asparagopsis anymore , I moved away from it in a different direction , " he said . <p> However , the sense of urgency and desperation which underpins the enthusiasm around asparagopsis is a good thing , Mr Camer-Pesci said . <p> " I think people are n't desperate enough , they should be jumping onto these things , but in a more sensible way , " he said . <p> " Maybe because of how desperate people are , they 've jumped in a bit early , but I 'm OK with that because it 's better than not jumping in at all , it shows there is still funding put towards climate saving research . " <p> Perri is an agriculture journalist with Farm Weekly . Originally from Ballarat , Victoria , Perri developed a love of regional and rural news at a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the agricultural industry . @@105485737 <p> A paradox lies at the heart of Australian public opinion about climate change . While there is clear general support for substantial government action to achieve net zero emissions by 2050 , there is also strong concern about the local impacts of new renewable energy infrastructure . <p> The rise of protest groups in regional Australia objecting to the installation of wind farms and transmission lines , in particular , presents a serious challenge to the Albanese government in communicating the importance of the net zero transition to the public . <p> In principle it seems that Australians strongly support this transition . In a poll for the Australia Institute 's Climate of the Nation report last year , two in three respondents went as far as to say our country should be a world leader in climate action . <p> Similarly , a 2020 Monash University study found eight out of ten Australians think the shift to renewable energy is inevitable , and two-thirds think we should be exporting renewable energy . <p> Studies also show strong support for renewable energy production through solar farms , with 90% of Australians prepared to live within ten kilometres of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a Guardian Essential poll from October last year found about 70% of respondents supported solar farms and 60% supported both offshore and onshore wind farms . <h> Local opposition to the net zero transition <p> But at the same time the installation of these same wind farms has attracted strong local protests , especially in NSW and Victoria . There has also been significant community pushback to the Australian Energy Market Operator 's plan to install 10,000 kilometres of overground transmission lines , which are key to carrying renewable energy to the electricity grid . Only 35% of respondents in the Guardian Essential poll supported them . <p> In an equally serious challenge for the net zero transition , 70% of respondents felt renewable energy should not be developed " at the expense of local communities " . <p> In a bid to address these concerns , the Australian Energy Market Commission has drafted new community engagement rules to ensure communities are involved early in the process of designing the routes of transmission lines . <p> The changes seek to ensure all stakeholders get information about a project in a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ can best play a role ) and have opportunities to be regularly involved throughout the planning of projects . <p> Wind energy is supported by most Australians , but community engagement is crucial when choosing appropriate designs and placements . Alex Eckermann , Unsplash <h> A more nuanced look at public opinion <p> There might be an even bigger issue that helps to explain the tension between apparent general support for acting to arrest global warming and local opposition to specific renewable energy projects . Segmentation studies , first undertaken in 2008 by researchers at Yale and George Mason Universities in the United States , and replicated in many countries , including Australia , are valuable for explaining this apparent paradox . <p> These studies break down views on climate change into segments of the population with different levels of concern . The original research divided American popular opinion into six groups : Alarmed , Concerned , Cautious , Disengaged , Doubtful and Dismissive . <p> The spectrum ranges from those most worried about climate change and who tend to take the most action , both in their personal lives @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ climate change is happening , or feel it is n't something we need to address . <p> A range of segmentation studies conducted in 2011 , 2016 , 2020and 2022 by researchers from both Australia and the US show Australians have become more worried over time . The Alarmed segment more than doubled between 2011 and 2022 . <p> Australians have become more alarmed about climate change over time . Note the Alert segment from the 2022 Climate Compass has been included in the Alarmed segment to allow for comparison , as this sub-segment was not separated out in the earlier studies . Adapted from information published by Australian National University and Edith Cowan University researchers and by The Sunrise Project <p> However , the studies show different segments of the population have different views of when we need to act to arrest climate change . <p> For example , the 2022 Climate Compass report highlights that people in the Concerned segment -- the largest group , comprising one in four Australians -- feel climate change is a serious problem but that its impacts will be most felt by future @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ much more urgent problem . <p> While the polling data do not show whether many protesters against renewable energy projects belong to the Concerned segment of the population , it remains essential to explore communication strategies that might move the large numbers of Australians who identify as Concerned into the Alarmed camp . One way to do this might be on the nightly TV news . <h> Looking more closely at the weather <p> From flooding to heat , many Australian extreme weather records have been broken in recent years . Some people feel that is part and parcel of living in Australia , and remain unaware of the connection between climate change and the increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather . And it can be difficult to translate complex climate phenomena into terms the public can easily understand . <p> Yet while the research is not conclusive , there is good evidence that floods , fires and heat waves increase popular concern about global warming by exposing the connection between extreme weather and climate change . Research from our centre argues that weather presenters can play a greater @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ presenters , along with climate scientists , farmers and fire fighters , as the most trusted sources of information about climate change . <h> Looking ahead <p> Whether or not we fully understand the ways climate change is impacting our society , most Australians realise it 's only going to get worse unless we do something about it . We 've now moved into the implementation phase of the net zero transition , but many doubt that we 'll achieve it in time . The Guardian Essential poll showed just 31% of respondents felt it was " very " or " quite " likely Australia would achieve its net zero target . <p> Therefore , it is crucial that we ramp up action towards transforming our energy sector , but it 's equally important that communities be part of deciding how this is achieved . In doing so , we can improve public support , and bring net zero within reach . <p> Climate protestors at World Environment Day , Sydney 2023 .
